ABSTRACT BASE CLASSES WITH GENERIC TYPES\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom abc import ABC, abstractmethod\nfrom typing import TypeVar, Generic\n\nT = TypeVar('T')\n\nclass Container(ABC, Generic[T]):\n    @abstractmethod\n    def get(self) -> T:\n        pass\n    \n    @abstractmethod\n    def set(self, value: T) -> None:\n        pass\n\nclass BoxContainer(Container[str]):\n    def __init__(self):\n        self._value: str = \"\"\n    \n    def get(self) -> str:\n        return self._value\n    \n    def set(self, value: str) -> None:\n        self._value = value\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Abstract class with generic type parameter\nabstract class Container[T] {\n  def get: T\n  def set(value: T): Unit\n}\n\n// Concrete implementation\nclass BoxContainer extends Container[String] {\n  private var value: String = \"\"\n  \n  def get: String = value\n  def set(v: String): Unit = {\n    value = v\n  }\n}\n\n// Better: trait-based (more idiomatic)\ntrait Container[T] {\n  def get: T\n  def set(value: T): Unit\n}\n\n// Immutable version (preferred)\ntrait ImmutableContainer[T] {\n  def get: T\n  def withValue(value: T): ImmutableContainer[T]\n}\n\ncase class BoxContainer(value: String) extends ImmutableContainer[String] {\n  def get: String = value\n  def withValue(v: String): ImmutableContainer[String] = copy(value = v)\n}\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Use `trait` for interfaces (more flexible than abstract classes)\n- Generic types use `[T]` syntax, same as Python's `Generic[T]`\n- Prefer immutable implementations with `.copy()` and case classes\n- Use companion objects for factory methods with `apply`\n\n---\n\n## 2. UNION TYPES & OPTIONAL HANDLING\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom typing import Union, Optional\n\ndef process_data(value: Union[str, int, None]) -> Optional[str]:\n    if value is None:\n        return None\n    elif isinstance(value, str):\n        return value.upper()\n    elif isinstance(value, int):\n        return str(value * 2)\n    return None\n\n# Better with Optional\ndef find_user(user_id: int) -> Optional[User]:\n    user = db.get(user_id)\n    return user if user else None\n\ndef get_user_email(user_id: int) -> Optional[str]:\n    user = find_user(user_id)\n    if user is None:\n        return None\n    return user.email\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Union types using sealed traits\nsealed trait Value\ncase class StringValue(s: String) extends Value\ncase class IntValue(n: Int) extends Value\ncase object NullValue extends Value\n\ndef processData(value: Value): Option[String] = value match {\n  case StringValue(s) => Some(s.toUpperCase)\n  case IntValue(n) => Some((n * 2).toString)\n  case NullValue => None\n}\n\n// Using Option (standard for null safety)\ndef findUser(userId: Int): Option[User] = db.get(userId)\n\n// Chaining operations with flatMap\ndef getUserEmail(userId: Int): Option[String] =\n  findUser(userId).flatMap(_.email)\n\n// Using for-comprehension (cleaner)\ndef getUserEmail(userId: Int): Option[String] = for {\n  user <- findUser(userId)\n  email <- user.email\n} yield email\n\n// Either for error handling (like Union with error info)\nsealed trait Error\ncase class UserNotFound(id: Int) extends Error\ncase class InvalidEmail(reason: String) extends Error\n\ndef getUserEmailEither(userId: Int): Either[Error, String] = for {\n  user <- findUser(userId).toRight(UserNotFound(userId))\n  email <- Json.parse(json).as[User]\n}\n\nparseJson(jsonStr) match {\n  case Success(user) => println(s\"Got user: $user\")\n  case Failure(e) => println(s\"Error: ${e.getMessage}\")\n}\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Use `Option[T]` for nullable values (replaces `Optional[T]`)\n- Use `Either[Error, T]` for operations that can fail with error info\n- Use `Try[T]` for exception-based error handling\n- Chain operations with `flatMap` or use for-comprehension for readability\n- Sealed traits replace Python Union types for type-safe pattern matching\n\n---\n\n## 3. PROTOCOL CLASSES (STRUCTURAL TYPING)\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom typing import Protocol\n\nclass Drawable(Protocol):\n    def draw(self) -> str:\n        ...\n\nclass Circle:\n    def draw(self) -> str:\n        return \"Drawing circle\"\n\nclass Square:\n    def draw(self) -> str:\n        return \"Drawing square\"\n\ndef render(drawable: Drawable) -> None:\n    print(drawable.draw())\n\nrender(Circle())  # Works! Structural typing\nrender(Square())  # Works!\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Structural typing (not recommended - use traits instead)\ndef render(drawable: { def draw(): String }): Unit = {\n  println(drawable.draw())\n}\n\n// Better: Use traits (explicit contracts)\ntrait Drawable {\n  def draw(): String\n}\n\nclass Circle extends Drawable {\n  def draw(): String = \"Drawing circle\"\n}\n\nclass Square extends Drawable {\n  def draw(): String = \"Drawing square\"\n}\n\ndef render(drawable: Drawable): Unit = {\n  println(drawable.draw())\n}\n\n// Type classes for implicit structural typing (advanced)\ntrait Renderer[T] {\n  def render(t: T): String\n}\n\nimplicit val circleRenderer: Renderer[Circle] = (c: Circle) =>\n  \"Drawing circle\"\n\nimplicit val squareRenderer: Renderer[Square] = (s: Square) =>\n  \"Drawing square\"\n\ndef render[T: Renderer](t: T): Unit = {\n  val renderer = implicitly[Renderer[T]]\n  println(renderer.render(t))\n}\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Scala prefers explicit traits over structural typing\n- Structural typing with `{ def method() }` is supported but slower at runtime\n- Type classes provide more flexible ad-hoc polymorphism\n- Traits with inheritance are idiomatic and compiler-checked\n\n---\n\n## 4. TYPE VARIABLES WITH CONSTRAINTS\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom typing import TypeVar, Generic, Union\n\nT = TypeVar('T')\nT_co = TypeVar('T_co', covariant=True)\nT_contra = TypeVar('T_contra', contravariant=True)\n\n# Bounded type variable\nComparable = TypeVar('Comparable', int, str, float)\n\nclass Comparator(Generic[Comparable]):\n    def compare(self, a: Comparable, b: Comparable) -> int:\n        if a < b:\n            return -1\n        elif a > b:\n            return 1\n        return 0\n\n# Upper bound\nTNumeric = TypeVar('TNumeric', bound='Numeric')\n\nclass NumericProcessor(Generic[TNumeric]):\n    def double(self, value: TNumeric) -> TNumeric:\n        return value * 2  # type: ignore\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Basic type variable\ntrait Container[T] {\n  def get: T\n}\n\n// Covariance: Container[Dog] is Container[Animal]\ntrait Producer[+T] {\n  def produce: T\n}\n\n// Contravariance: Consumer[Animal] is Consumer[Dog]\ntrait Consumer[-T] {\n  def consume(t: T): Unit\n}\n\n// Bounded type variable (upper bound)\ntrait Comparable[T <: Comparable[T]] {\n  def compare(other: T): Int\n}\n\ncase class Person(name: String) extends Comparable[Person] {\n  def compare(other: Person): Int = name.compare(other.name)\n}\n\n// Multiple upper bounds with trait intersection\ntrait Numeric {\n  def +(other: Numeric): Numeric\n  def *(other: Numeric): Numeric\n}\n\ntrait Ordered[T] {\n  def compare(other: T): Int\n}\n\ndef findMax[T <: Ordered[T]](list: List[T]): Option[T] = {\n  list.maxByOption(_.compare(_))\n}\n\n// View bounds (implicit conversion, Scala 2)\ndef processNumeric[T <% Numeric](value: T): Numeric = value\n\n// Context bounds (implicit evidence, Scala 2)\ndef processOrdered[T: Ordering](list: List[T]): List[T] =\n  list.sorted\n\n// Multiple bounds\ntrait SortableAndNumeric[T <: Ordered[T] with Numeric]\n\n// Type aliases for cleaner code\ntype Num = Int\ntype IntList = List[Int]\n\n// Local type variables in methods\ndef process[T](list: List[T])(f: T => T): List[T] =\n  list.map(f)\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Upper bounds: `T <: UpperBound` (subtype constraint)\n- Lower bounds: `T >: LowerBound` (supertype constraint, rare)\n- Covariance `+T`: producer-only, can be returned\n- Contravariance `-T`: consumer-only, can be accepted\n- Use trait intersection with `with` for multiple bounds\n- Context bounds `[T: Typeclass]` implicit evidence\n- Type aliases simplify complex generic signatures\n\n---\n\n## 5. ENUMS & IMMUTABLE DATACLASSES\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom enum import Enum, auto\nfrom dataclasses import dataclass\n\nclass Status(Enum):\n    PENDING = auto()\n    APPROVED = auto()\n    REJECTED = auto()\n\nclass Color(Enum):\n    RED = \"red\"\n    GREEN = \"green\"\n    BLUE = \"blue\"\n\n@dataclass(frozen=True)\nclass User:\n    id: int\n    name: str\n    email: str\n    status: Status = Status.PENDING\n\n@dataclass(frozen=True)\nclass Address:\n    street: str\n    city: str\n    zipcode: str\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Scala 2 - sealed trait pattern for enums\nsealed trait Status\nobject Status {\n  case object Pending extends Status\n  case object Approved extends Status\n  case object Rejected extends Status\n}\n\n// Enum with associated values\nsealed trait Color {\n  def value: String\n}\nobject Color {\n  case object Red extends Color { val value = \"red\" }\n  case object Green extends Color { val value = \"green\" }\n  case object Blue extends Color { val value = \"blue\" }\n}\n\n// Immutable dataclasses → case classes\ncase class User(\n  id: Int,\n  name: String,\n  email: String,\n  status: Status = Status.Pending\n)\n\ncase class Address(\n  street: String,\n  city: String,\n  zipcode: String\n)\n\n// Using them\nval user = User(1, \"Alice\", \"alice@example.com\")\nval updated = user.copy(status = Status.Approved)\n\n// Extracting values in pattern matching\nuser match {\n  case User(id, name, email, Status.Approved) =>\n    println(s\"$name approved\")\n  case User(_, _, _, Status.Pending) =>\n    println(\"Waiting for approval\")\n  case _ => ()\n}\n\n// Equality, hashCode, toString auto-generated\nprintln(user)  // User(1,Alice,alice@example.com,Pending)\nprintln(user == user.copy())  // true\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Case classes = immutable dataclasses (auto-generates equals, hashCode, toString, copy)\n- Sealed traits + case objects = enums with pattern matching\n- Use `.copy(field = newValue)` to create modified copies\n- Companion objects provide factory methods\n- Pattern matching on case classes is type-safe and exhaustive\n\n---\n\n## 6. HIGHER-KINDED TYPES (FUNCTORS & MONADS)\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom typing import TypeVar, Generic, Callable\nfrom abc import ABC, abstractmethod\n\nT = TypeVar('T')\nU = TypeVar('U')\n\nclass Functor(ABC, Generic[T]):\n    @abstractmethod\n    def map(self, f: Callable[[T], U]) -> 'Functor[U]':\n        pass\n\nclass Monad(Functor[T]):\n    @abstractmethod\n    def flatMap(self, f: Callable[[T], 'Monad[U]']) -> 'Monad[U]':\n        pass\n    \n    @abstractmethod\n    def pure(self, value: T) -> 'Monad[T]':\n        pass\n\nclass Box(Monad[T]):\n    def __init__(self, value: T):\n        self._value = value\n    \n    def map(self, f: Callable[[T], U]) -> 'Box[U]':\n        return Box(f(self._value))\n    \n    def flatMap(self, f: Callable[[T], 'Box[U]']) -> 'Box[U]':\n        return f(self._value)\n    \n    def pure(self, value: T) -> 'Box[T]':\n        return Box(value)\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Functor trait (from cats library or custom)\ntrait Functor[F[_]] {\n  def map[A, B](fa: F[A])(f: A => B): F[B]\n}\n\n// Monad trait\ntrait Monad[F[_]] extends Functor[F] {\n  def flatMap[A, B](fa: F[A])(f: A => F[B]): F[B]\n  def pure[A](a: A): F[A]\n  \n  // Derived\n  override def map[A, B](fa: F[A])(f: A => B): F[B] =\n    flatMap(fa)(a => pure(f(a)))\n}\n\n// Custom Box monad\ncase class Box[A](value: A)\n\nimplicit val boxMonad: Monad[Box] = new Monad[Box] {\n  def flatMap[A, B](fa: Box[A])(f: A => Box[B]): Box[B] =\n    f(fa.value)\n  \n  def pure[A](a: A): Box[A] = Box(a)\n}\n\n// Usage\nval box = Box(5)\nval result = box.flatMap(n => Box(n * 2))  // Box(10)\n\n// Better: use standard library types that already implement these\n\n// Option is a monad\nval opt: Option[Int] = Some(5)\nval result = opt.flatMap(n => Some(n * 2))  // Some(10)\n\n// List is a functor and monad\nval list = List(1, 2, 3)\nval doubled = list.map(_ * 2)  // List(2, 4, 6)\nval flattened = list.flatMap(n => List(n, n * 2))  // List(1,2,2,4,3,6)\n\n// Using cats library (recommended)\nimport cats._\nimport cats.implicits._\n\n// Syntax extension\nval result = Box(5).flatMap(n => Box(n * 2))\n\n// For-comprehension (desugars to flatMap/map)\nval computed = for {\n  x <- Box(5)\n  y <- Box(x * 2)\n  z <- Box(y + 3)\n} yield z  // Box(13)\n\n// Traverse and sequence with cats\nval listOfOptions: List[Option[Int]] = List(Some(1), Some(2), Some(3))\nval result: Option[List[Int]] = listOfOptions.sequence\n// Some(List(1, 2, 3))\n\n// Applicative functor\ncase class User(id: Int, name: String)\n\nval userId: Option[Int] = Some(1)\nval userName: Option[String] = Some(\"Alice\")\n\nval user = (userId, userName).mapN(User.apply)\n// Some(User(1, Alice))\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Use higher-kinded types with `F[_]` syntax (F is a type constructor)\n- Scala's standard types (Option, List, Future) already implement Functor/Monad\n- For-comprehensions desugar to flatMap/map chains\n- Cats library provides unified interfaces and syntax extensions\n- Use `.sequence` to flip container types: `List[Option[A]]` → `Option[List[A]]`\n- Use `.mapN` (applicative) for combining independent effects\n\n---\n\n## 7. MUTABLE & IMMUTABLE COLLECTIONS WITH GENERICS\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom typing import List, Dict, Set, Tuple\nfrom collections import defaultdict\n\n# Lists\nmutable_list: List[int] = [1, 2, 3]\nmutable_list.append(4)\nmutable_list.extend([5, 6])\n\n# Tuples (immutable)\nimmutable_tuple: Tuple[int, str, float] = (1, \"hello\", 3.14)\n\n# Dicts\nmutable_dict: Dict[str, int] = {\"a\": 1, \"b\": 2}\nmutable_dict[\"c\"] = 3\n\n# Sets\nmutable_set: Set[int] = {1, 2, 3}\nmutable_set.add(4)\n\n# Comprehensions\nsquared = [x**2 for x in mutable_list]\nfiltered = [x for x in mutable_list if x > 2]\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\nimport scala.collection.mutable\nimport scala.collection.immutable\n\n// Immutable by default (preferred)\nval immutableList: List[Int] = List(1, 2, 3)\nval withFour = immutableList :+ 4  // Creates new list\nval withMore = immutableList ++ List(5, 6)  // Creates new list\n\n// Mutable collections (when needed)\nval mutableList: mutable.ListBuffer[Int] = mutable.ListBuffer(1, 2, 3)\nmutableList += 4  // Modifies in place\nmutableList ++= List(5, 6)\n\n// Tuples (immutable, heterogeneous)\nval tuple: (Int, String, Double) = (1, \"hello\", 3.14)\nval (a, b, c) = tuple  // Destructuring\n\n// Maps (immutable by default)\nval immutableMap: Map[String, Int] = Map(\"a\" -> 1, \"b\" -> 2)\nval extended = immutableMap + (\"c\" -> 3)  // Creates new map\nval updated = immutableMap.updated(\"a\", 10)\n\n// Mutable maps\nval mutableMap: mutable.Map[String, Int] = mutable.Map(\"a\" -> 1)\nmutableMap(\"c\") = 3  // Modifies in place\n\n// Sets (immutable by default)\nval immutableSet: Set[Int] = Set(1, 2, 3)\nval withFour = immutableSet + 4\nval withoutTwo = immutableSet - 2\n\n// Mutable sets\nval mutableSet: mutable.Set[Int] = mutable.Set(1, 2, 3)\nmutableSet += 4\n\n// Collection transformations (work on both mutable and immutable)\nval squared = immutableList.map(x => x * x)\nval filtered = immutableList.filter(_ > 2)\nval flattened = List(List(1, 2), List(3, 4)).flatten\n\n// For-comprehensions\nval result = for {\n  x <- immutableList\n  if x > 2\n} yield x * x  // List(9, 16, 25) - maps to filter + map\n\n// Aggregations\nval sum = immutableList.sum\nval product = immutableList.foldLeft(1)(_ * _)\nval grouped = immutableList.groupBy(_ % 2)  // Map(1 -> List(1, 3), 0 -> List(2, 4))\n\n// Sorting\nval sorted = immutableList.sorted\nval sortedDesc = immutableList.sorted.reverse\nval sortedBy = List(\"apple\", \"pie\", \"a\").sortBy(_.length)\n\n// Zipping\nval zipped = List(1, 2, 3).zip(List(\"a\", \"b\", \"c\"))\n// List((1,a), (2,b), (3,c))\n\n// Finding elements\nval firstEven = immutableList.find(_ % 2 == 0)  // Option[Int]\nval allEven = immutableList.forall(_ % 2 == 0)  // Boolean\nval anyEven = immutableList.exists(_ % 2 == 0)  // Boolean\n\n// Converting between mutable/immutable\nval immToMut = immutableList.to(mutable.ListBuffer)\nval mutToImm = mutableList.toList\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Scala defaults to immutable collections (prefer this)\n- Mutable collections in `scala.collection.mutable` package when needed\n- Use `val` for variables (immutable reference)\n- Use `var` rarely (mutable reference)\n- Chain operations for functional style (map, filter, fold)\n- For-comprehensions are syntactic sugar for chain operations\n- Tuples support destructuring in pattern matching\n\n---\n\n## 8. BUILDER PATTERN WITH FLUENT INTERFACE\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nclass QueryBuilder:\n    def __init__(self):\n        self._select = []\n        self._from = None\n        self._where = []\n        self._limit = None\n    \n    def select(self, *fields):\n        self._select.extend(fields)\n        return self\n    \n    def from_table(self, table):\n        self._from = table\n        return self\n    \n    def where(self, condition):\n        self._where.append(condition)\n        return self\n    \n    def limit(self, count):\n        self._limit = count\n        return self\n    \n    def build(self):\n        query = f\"SELECT {', '.join(self._select)}\"\n        if self._from:\n            query += f\" FROM {self._from}\"\n        for w in self._where:\n            query += f\" WHERE {w}\"\n        if self._limit:\n            query += f\" LIMIT {self._limit}\"\n        return query\n\nbuilder = QueryBuilder()\nquery = (builder\n    .select(\"id\", \"name\")\n    .from_table(\"users\")\n    .where(\"age > 18\")\n    .limit(10)\n    .build())\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Immutable builder with case class\ncase class QueryBuilder(\n  select: List[String] = Nil,\n  from: Option[String] = None,\n  where: List[String] = Nil,\n  limit: Option[Int] = None\n) {\n  def select(fields: String*): QueryBuilder =\n    copy(select = select ++ fields)\n  \n  def from(table: String): QueryBuilder =\n    copy(from = Some(table))\n  \n  def where(condition: String): QueryBuilder =\n    copy(where = where :+ condition)\n  \n  def limit(count: Int): QueryBuilder =\n    copy(limit = Some(count))\n  \n  def build: String = {\n    val parts = scala.collection.mutable.ListBuffer[String]()\n    parts += s\"SELECT ${select.mkString(\", \")}\"\n    from.foreach(t => parts += s\"FROM $t\")\n    where.foreach(w => parts += s\"WHERE $w\")\n    limit.foreach(l => parts += s\"LIMIT $l\")\n    parts.mkString(\" \")\n  }\n}\n\n// Usage\nval query = QueryBuilder()\n  .select(\"id\", \"name\")\n  .from(\"users\")\n  .where(\"age > 18\")\n  .limit(10)\n  .build\n// SELECT id, name FROM users WHERE age > 18 LIMIT 10\n\n// Alternative: using companion object with apply\nobject QueryBuilder {\n  def apply(): QueryBuilder = new QueryBuilder()\n  \n  def select(fields: String*): QueryBuilder =\n    QueryBuilder().select(fields: _*)\n}\n\n// Advanced: using implicit classes for DSL\nimplicit class QueryDSL(val self: QueryBuilder) {\n  def and(condition: String): QueryBuilder =\n    self.where(s\"${self.where.last} AND $condition\")\n}\n\nval query = QueryBuilder()\n  .select(\"id\", \"name\")\n  .from(\"users\")\n  .where(\"age > 18\")\n  .and(\"status = 'active'\")\n  .build\n\n// Best practice: sealed case class hierarchy for query types\nsealed trait Query {\n  def build: String\n}\n\ncase class SelectQuery(\n  fields: List[String],\n  table: String,\n  conditions: List[String] = Nil,\n  limitCount: Option[Int] = None\n) extends Query {\n  def build: String = {\n    val parts = scala.collection.mutable.ListBuffer[String]()\n    parts += s\"SELECT ${fields.mkString(\", \")}\"\n    parts += s\"FROM $table\"\n    conditions.foreach(c => parts += s\"WHERE $c\")\n    limitCount.foreach(l => parts += s\"LIMIT $l\")\n    parts.mkString(\" \")\n  }\n}\n\n// Fluent builder for sealed types\nclass QueryBuilder {\n  private var fields: List[String] = Nil\n  private var table: Option[String] = None\n  private var conditions: List[String] = Nil\n  private var limitCount: Option[Int] = None\n  \n  def select(f: String*): this.type = {\n    fields = f.toList\n    this\n  }\n  \n  def from(t: String): this.type = {\n    table = Some(t)\n    this\n  }\n  \n  def where(c: String): this.type = {\n    conditions :+= c\n    this\n  }\n  \n  def limit(n: Int): this.type = {\n    limitCount = Some(n)\n    this\n  }\n  \n  def build: SelectQuery = {\n    SelectQuery(\n      fields = fields,\n      table = table.getOrElse(\"\"),\n      conditions = conditions,\n      limitCount = limitCount\n    )\n  }\n}\n\n// Usage\nval query = new QueryBuilder()\n  .select(\"id\", \"name\")\n  .from(\"users\")\n  .where(\"age > 18\")\n  .limit(10)\n  .build\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Use immutable case classes with `.copy()` for the builder (preferred)\n- Use `this.type` return type for mutable builders to preserve self type\n- Companion objects provide factory methods and default instances\n- For-comprehensions and implicit classes enable DSL-like syntax\n- Sealed trait hierarchies ensure exhaustive pattern matching\n- Builder methods should return `this` or modified copy for chaining\n\n---\n\n## BONUS: COMMON LIBRARIES FOR SCALA 2.13\n\n| Feature | Python | Scala ABSTRACT BASE CLASSES WITH GENERIC TYPES\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom abc import ABC, abstractmethod\nfrom typing import TypeVar, Generic\n\nT = TypeVar('T')\n\nclass Container(ABC, Generic[T]):\n    @abstractmethod\n    def get(self) -> T:\n        pass\n    \n    @abstractmethod\n    def set(self, value: T) -> None:\n        pass\n\nclass BoxContainer(Container[str]):\n    def __init__(self):\n        self._value: str = \"\"\n    \n    def get(self) -> str:\n        return self._value\n    \n    def set(self, value: str) -> None:\n        self._value = value\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Abstract class with generic type parameter\nabstract class Container[T] {\n  def get: T\n  def set(value: T): Unit\n}\n\n// Concrete implementation\nclass BoxContainer extends Container[String] {\n  private var value: String = \"\"\n  \n  def get: String = value\n  def set(v: String): Unit = {\n    value = v\n  }\n}\n\n// Better: trait-based (more idiomatic)\ntrait Container[T] {\n  def get: T\n  def set(value: T): Unit\n}\n\n// Immutable version (preferred)\ntrait ImmutableContainer[T] {\n  def get: T\n  def withValue(value: T): ImmutableContainer[T]\n}\n\ncase class BoxContainer(value: String) extends ImmutableContainer[String] {\n  def get: String = value\n  def withValue(v: String): ImmutableContainer[String] = copy(value = v)\n}\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Use `trait` for interfaces (more flexible than abstract classes)\n- Generic types use `[T]` syntax, same as Python's `Generic[T]`\n- Prefer immutable implementations with `.copy()` and case classes\n- Use companion objects for factory methods with `apply`\n\n---\n\n## 2. UNION TYPES & OPTIONAL HANDLING\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom typing import Union, Optional\n\ndef process_data(value: Union[str, int, None]) -> Optional[str]:\n    if value is None:\n        return None\n    elif isinstance(value, str):\n        return value.upper()\n    elif isinstance(value, int):\n        return str(value * 2)\n    return None\n\n# Better with Optional\ndef find_user(user_id: int) -> Optional[User]:\n    user = db.get(user_id)\n    return user if user else None\n\ndef get_user_email(user_id: int) -> Optional[str]:\n    user = find_user(user_id)\n    if user is None:\n        return None\n    return user.email\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Union types using sealed traits\nsealed trait Value\ncase class StringValue(s: String) extends Value\ncase class IntValue(n: Int) extends Value\ncase object NullValue extends Value\n\ndef processData(value: Value): Option[String] = value match {\n  case StringValue(s) => Some(s.toUpperCase)\n  case IntValue(n) => Some((n * 2).toString)\n  case NullValue => None\n}\n\n// Using Option (standard for null safety)\ndef findUser(userId: Int): Option[User] = db.get(userId)\n\n// Chaining operations with flatMap\ndef getUserEmail(userId: Int): Option[String] =\n  findUser(userId).flatMap(_.email)\n\n// Using for-comprehension (cleaner)\ndef getUserEmail(userId: Int): Option[String] = for {\n  user <- findUser(userId)\n  email <- user.email\n} yield email\n\n// Either for error handling (like Union with error info)\nsealed trait Error\ncase class UserNotFound(id: Int) extends Error\ncase class InvalidEmail(reason: String) extends Error\n\ndef getUserEmailEither(userId: Int): Either[Error, String] = for {\n  user <- findUser(userId).toRight(UserNotFound(userId))\n  email <- user.email.toRight(InvalidEmail(\"No email in profile\"))\n} yield email\n\n// Try for exceptions\nimport scala.util.{Try, Success, Failure}\n\ndef parseJson(json: String): Try[User] = Try {\n  Json.parse(json).as[User]\n}\n\nparseJson(jsonStr) match {\n  case Success(user) => println(s\"Got user: $user\")\n  case Failure(e) => println(s\"Error: ${e.getMessage}\")\n}\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Use `Option[T]` for nullable values (replaces `Optional[T]`)\n- Use `Either[Error, T]` for operations that can fail with error info\n- Use `Try[T]` for exception-based error handling\n- Chain operations with `flatMap` or use for-comprehension for readability\n- Sealed traits replace Python Union types for type-safe pattern matching\n\n---\n\n## 3. PROTOCOL CLASSES (STRUCTURAL TYPING)\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom typing import Protocol\n\nclass Drawable(Protocol):\n    def draw(self) -> str:\n        ...\n\nclass Circle:\n    def draw(self) -> str:\n        return \"Drawing circle\"\n\nclass Square:\n    def draw(self) -> str:\n        return \"Drawing square\"\n\ndef render(drawable: Drawable) -> None:\n    print(drawable.draw())\n\nrender(Circle())  # Works! Structural typing\nrender(Square())  # Works!\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Structural typing (not recommended - use traits instead)\ndef render(drawable: { def draw(): String }): Unit = {\n  println(drawable.draw())\n}\n\n// Better: Use traits (explicit contracts)\ntrait Drawable {\n  def draw(): String\n}\n\nclass Circle extends Drawable {\n  def draw(): String = \"Drawing circle\"\n}\n\nclass Square extends Drawable {\n  def draw(): String = \"Drawing square\"\n}\n\ndef render(drawable: Drawable): Unit = {\n  println(drawable.draw())\n}\n\n// Type classes for implicit structural typing (advanced)\ntrait Renderer[T] {\n  def render(t: T): String\n}\n\nimplicit val circleRenderer: Renderer[Circle] = (c: Circle) =>\n  \"Drawing circle\"\n\nimplicit val squareRenderer: Renderer[Square] = (s: Square) =>\n  \"Drawing square\"\n\ndef render[T: Renderer](t: T): Unit = {\n  val renderer = implicitly[Renderer[T]]\n  println(renderer.render(t))\n}\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Scala prefers explicit traits over structural typing\n- Structural typing with `{ def method() }` is supported but slower at runtime\n- Type classes provide more flexible ad-hoc polymorphism\n- Traits with inheritance are idiomatic and compiler-checked\n\n---\n\n## 4. TYPE VARIABLES WITH CONSTRAINTS\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom typing import TypeVar, Generic, Union\n\nT = TypeVar('T')\nT_co = TypeVar('T_co', covariant=True)\nT_contra = TypeVar('T_contra', contravariant=True)\n\n# Bounded type variable\nComparable = TypeVar('Comparable', int, str, float)\n\nclass Comparator(Generic[Comparable]):\n    def compare(self, a: Comparable, b: Comparable) -> int:\n        if a < b:\n            return -1\n        elif a > b:\n            return 1\n        return 0\n\n# Upper bound\nTNumeric = TypeVar('TNumeric', bound='Numeric')\n\nclass NumericProcessor(Generic[TNumeric]):\n    def double(self, value: TNumeric) -> TNumeric:\n        return value * 2  # type: ignore\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Basic type variable\ntrait Container[T] {\n  def get: T\n}\n\n// Covariance: Container[Dog] is Container[Animal]\ntrait Producer[+T] {\n  def produce: T\n}\n\n// Contravariance: Consumer[Animal] is Consumer[Dog]\ntrait Consumer[-T] {\n  def consume(t: T): Unit\n}\n\n// Bounded type variable (upper bound)\ntrait Comparable[T <: Comparable[T]] {\n  def compare(other: T): Int\n}\n\ncase class Person(name: String) extends Comparable[Person] {\n  def compare(other: Person): Int = name.compare(other.name)\n}\n\n// Multiple upper bounds with trait intersection\ntrait Numeric {\n  def +(other: Numeric): Numeric\n  def *(other: Numeric): Numeric\n}\n\ntrait Ordered[T] {\n  def compare(other: T): Int\n}\n\ndef findMax[T <: Ordered[T]](list: List[T]): Option[T] = {\n  list.maxByOption(_.compare(_))\n}\n\n// View bounds (implicit conversion, Scala 2)\ndef processNumeric[T <% Numeric](value: T): Numeric = value\n\n// Context bounds (implicit evidence, Scala 2)\ndef processOrdered[T: Ordering](list: List[T]): List[T] =\n  list.sorted\n\n// Multiple bounds\ntrait SortableAndNumeric[T <: Ordered[T] with Numeric]\n\n// Type aliases for cleaner code\ntype Num = Int\ntype IntList = List[Int]\n\n// Local type variables in methods\ndef process[T](list: List[T])(f: T => T): List[T] =\n  list.map(f)\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Upper bounds: `T <: UpperBound` (subtype constraint)\n- Lower bounds: `T >: LowerBound` (supertype constraint, rare)\n- Covariance `+T`: producer-only, can be returned\n- Contravariance `-T`: consumer-only, can be accepted\n- Use trait intersection with `with` for multiple bounds\n- Context bounds `[T: Typeclass]` implicit evidence\n- Type aliases simplify complex generic signatures\n\n---\n\n## 5. ENUMS & IMMUTABLE DATACLASSES\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom enum import Enum, auto\nfrom dataclasses import dataclass\n\nclass Status(Enum):\n    PENDING = auto()\n    APPROVED = auto()\n    REJECTED = auto()\n\nclass Color(Enum):\n    RED = \"red\"\n    GREEN = \"green\"\n    BLUE = \"blue\"\n\n@dataclass(frozen=True)\nclass User:\n    id: int\n    name: str\n    email: str\n    status: Status = Status.PENDING\n\n@dataclass(frozen=True)\nclass Address:\n    street: str\n    city: str\n    zipcode: str\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Scala 2 - sealed trait pattern for enums\nsealed trait Status\nobject Status {\n  case object Pending extends Status\n  case object Approved extends Status\n  case object Rejected extends Status\n}\n\n// Enum with associated values\nsealed trait Color {\n  def value: String\n}\nobject Color {\n  case object Red extends Color { val value = \"red\" }\n  case object Green extends Color { val value = \"green\" }\n  case object Blue extends Color { val value = \"blue\" }\n}\n\n// Immutable dataclasses → case classes\ncase class User(\n  id: Int,\n  name: String,\n  email: String,\n  status: Status = Status.Pending\n)\n\ncase class Address(\n  street: String,\n  city: String,\n  zipcode: String\n)\n\n// Using them\nval user = User(1, \"Alice\", \"alice@example.com\")\nval updated = user.copy(status = Status.Approved)\n\n// Extracting values in pattern matching\nuser match {\n  case User(id, name, email, Status.Approved) =>\n    println(s\"$name approved\")\n  case User(_, _, _, Status.Pending) =>\n    println(\"Waiting for approval\")\n  case _ => ()\n}\n\n// Equality, hashCode, toString auto-generated\nprintln(user)  // User(1,Alice,alice@example.com,Pending)\nprintln(user == user.copy())  // true\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Case classes = immutable dataclasses (auto-generates equals, hashCode, toString, copy)\n- Sealed traits + case objects = enums with pattern matching\n- Use `.copy(field = newValue)` to create modified copies\n- Companion objects provide factory methods\n- Pattern matching on case classes is type-safe and exhaustive\n\n---\n\n## 6. HIGHER-KINDED TYPES (FUNCTORS & MONADS)\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom typing import TypeVar, Generic, Callable\nfrom abc import ABC, abstractmethod\n\nT = TypeVar('T')\nU = TypeVar('U')\n\nclass Functor(ABC, Generic[T]):\n    @abstractmethod\n    def map(self, f: Callable[[T], U]) -> 'Functor[U]':\n        pass\n\nclass Monad(Functor[T]):\n    @abstractmethod\n    def flatMap(self, f: Callable[[T], 'Monad[U]']) -> 'Monad[U]':\n        pass\n    \n    @abstractmethod\n    def pure(self, value: T) -> 'Monad[T]':\n        pass\n\nclass Box(Monad[T]):\n    def __init__(self, value: T):\n        self._value = value\n    \n    def map(self, f: Callable[[T], U]) -> 'Box[U]':\n        return Box(f(self._value))\n    \n    def flatMap(self, f: Callable[[T], 'Box[U]']) -> 'Box[U]':\n        return f(self._value)\n    \n    def pure(self, value: T) -> 'Box[T]':\n        return Box(value)\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Functor trait (from cats library or custom)\ntrait Functor[F[_]] {\n  def map[A, B](fa: F[A])(f: A => B): F[B]\n}\n\n// Monad trait\ntrait Monad[F[_]] extends Functor[F] {\n  def flatMap[A, B](fa: F[A])(f: A => F[B]): F[B]\n  def pure[A](a: A): F[A]\n  \n  // Derived\n  override def map[A, B](fa: F[A])(f: A => B): F[B] =\n    flatMap(fa)(a => pure(f(a)))\n}\n\n// Custom Box monad\ncase class Box[A](value: A)\n\nimplicit val boxMonad: Monad[Box] = new Monad[Box] {\n  def flatMap[A, B](fa: Box[A])(f: A => Box[B]): Box[B] =\n    f(fa.value)\n  \n  def pure[A](a: A): Box[A] = Box(a)\n}\n\n// Usage\nval box = Box(5)\nval result = box.flatMap(n => Box(n * 2))  // Box(10)\n\n// Better: use standard library types that already implement these\n\n// Option is a monad\nval opt: Option[Int] = Some(5)\nval result = opt.flatMap(n => Some(n * 2))  // Some(10)\n\n// List is a functor and monad\nval list = List(1, 2, 3)\nval doubled = list.map(_ * 2)  // List(2, 4, 6)\nval flattened = list.flatMap(n => List(n, n * 2))  // List(1,2,2,4,3,6)\n\n// Using cats library (recommended)\nimport cats._\nimport cats.implicits._\n\n// Syntax extension\nval result = Box(5).flatMap(n => Box(n * 2))\n\n// For-comprehension (desugars to flatMap/map)\nval computed = for {\n  x <- Box(5)\n  y <- Box(x * 2)\n  z <- Box(y + 3)\n} yield z  // Box(13)\n\n// Traverse and sequence with cats\nval listOfOptions: List[Option[Int]] = List(Some(1), Some(2), Some(3))\nval result: Option[List[Int]] = listOfOptions.sequence\n// Some(List(1, 2, 3))\n\n// Applicative functor\ncase class User(id: Int, name: String)\n\nval userId: Option[Int] = Some(1)\nval userName: Option[String] = Some(\"Alice\")\n\nval user = (userId, userName).mapN(User.apply)\n// Some(User(1, Alice))\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Use higher-kinded types with `F[_]` syntax (F is a type constructor)\n- Scala's standard types (Option, List, Future) already implement Functor/Monad\n- For-comprehensions desugar to flatMap/map chains\n- Cats library provides unified interfaces and syntax extensions\n- Use `.sequence` to flip container types: `List[Option[A]]` → `Option[List[A]]`\n- Use `.mapN` (applicative) for combining independent effects\n\n---\n\n## 7. MUTABLE & IMMUTABLE COLLECTIONS WITH GENERICS\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nfrom typing import List, Dict, Set, Tuple\nfrom collections import defaultdict\n\n# Lists\nmutable_list: List[int] = [1, 2, 3]\nmutable_list.append(4)\nmutable_list.extend([5, 6])\n\n# Tuples (immutable)\nimmutable_tuple: Tuple[int, str, float] = (1, \"hello\", 3.14)\n\n# Dicts\nmutable_dict: Dict[str, int] = {\"a\": 1, \"b\": 2}\nmutable_dict[\"c\"] = 3\n\n# Sets\nmutable_set: Set[int] = {1, 2, 3}\nmutable_set.add(4)\n\n# Comprehensions\nsquared = [x**2 for x in mutable_list]\nfiltered = [x for x in mutable_list if x > 2]\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\nimport scala.collection.mutable\nimport scala.collection.immutable\n\n// Immutable by default (preferred)\nval immutableList: List[Int] = List(1, 2, 3)\nval withFour = immutableList :+ 4  // Creates new list\nval withMore = immutableList ++ List(5, 6)  // Creates new list\n\n// Mutable collections (when needed)\nval mutableList: mutable.ListBuffer[Int] = mutable.ListBuffer(1, 2, 3)\nmutableList += 4  // Modifies in place\nmutableList ++= List(5, 6)\n\n// Tuples (immutable, heterogeneous)\nval tuple: (Int, String, Double) = (1, \"hello\", 3.14)\nval (a, b, c) = tuple  // Destructuring\n\n// Maps (immutable by default)\nval immutableMap: Map[String, Int] = Map(\"a\" -> 1, \"b\" -> 2)\nval extended = immutableMap + (\"c\" -> 3)  // Creates new map\nval updated = immutableMap.updated(\"a\", 10)\n\n// Mutable maps\nval mutableMap: mutable.Map[String, Int] = mutable.Map(\"a\" -> 1)\nmutableMap(\"c\") = 3  // Modifies in place\n\n// Sets (immutable by default)\nval immutableSet: Set[Int] = Set(1, 2, 3)\nval withFour = immutableSet + 4\nval withoutTwo = immutableSet - 2\n\n// Mutable sets\nval mutableSet: mutable.Set[Int] = mutable.Set(1, 2, 3)\nmutableSet += 4\n\n// Collection transformations (work on both mutable and immutable)\nval squared = immutableList.map(x => x * x)\nval filtered = immutableList.filter(_ > 2)\nval flattened = List(List(1, 2), List(3, 4)).flatten\n\n// For-comprehensions\nval result = for {\n  x <- immutableList\n  if x > 2\n} yield x * x  // List(9, 16, 25) - maps to filter + map\n\n// Aggregations\nval sum = immutableList.sum\nval product = immutableList.foldLeft(1)(_ * _)\nval grouped = immutableList.groupBy(_ % 2)  // Map(1 -> List(1, 3), 0 -> List(2, 4))\n\n// Sorting\nval sorted = immutableList.sorted\nval sortedDesc = immutableList.sorted.reverse\nval sortedBy = List(\"apple\", \"pie\", \"a\").sortBy(_.length)\n\n// Zipping\nval zipped = List(1, 2, 3).zip(List(\"a\", \"b\", \"c\"))\n// List((1,a), (2,b), (3,c))\n\n// Finding elements\nval firstEven = immutableList.find(_ % 2 == 0)  // Option[Int]\nval allEven = immutableList.forall(_ % 2 == 0)  // Boolean\nval anyEven = immutableList.exists(_ % 2 == 0)  // Boolean\n\n// Converting between mutable/immutable\nval immToMut = immutableList.to(mutable.ListBuffer)\nval mutToImm = mutableList.toList\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Scala defaults to immutable collections (prefer this)\n- Mutable collections in `scala.collection.mutable` package when needed\n- Use `val` for variables (immutable reference)\n- Use `var` rarely (mutable reference)\n- Chain operations for functional style (map, filter, fold)\n- For-comprehensions are syntactic sugar for chain operations\n- Tuples support destructuring in pattern matching\n\n---\n\n## 8. BUILDER PATTERN WITH FLUENT INTERFACE\n\n### Python Pattern\n```python\nclass QueryBuilder:\n    def __init__(self):\n        self._select = []\n        self._from = None\n        self._where = []\n        self._limit = None\n    \n    def select(self, *fields):\n        self._select.extend(fields)\n        return self\n    \n    def from_table(self, table):\n        self._from = table\n        return self\n    \n    def where(self, condition):\n        self._where.append(condition)\n        return self\n    \n    def limit(self, count):\n        self._limit = count\n        return self\n    \n    def build(self):\n        query = f\"SELECT {', '.join(self._select)}\"\n        if self._from:\n            query += f\" FROM {self._from}\"\n        for w in self._where:\n            query += f\" WHERE {w}\"\n        if self._limit:\n            query += f\" LIMIT {self._limit}\"\n        return query\n\nbuilder = QueryBuilder()\nquery = (builder\n    .select(\"id\", \"name\")\n    .from_table(\"users\")\n    .where(\"age > 18\")\n    .limit(10)\n    .build())\n```\n\n### Scala 2.13 Approach\n\n```scala\n// Immutable builder with case class\ncase class QueryBuilder(\n  select: List[String] = Nil,\n  from: Option[String] = None,\n  where: List[String] = Nil,\n  limit: Option[Int] = None\n) {\n  def select(fields: String*): QueryBuilder =\n    copy(select = select ++ fields)\n  \n  def from(table: String): QueryBuilder =\n    copy(from = Some(table))\n  \n  def where(condition: String): QueryBuilder =\n    copy(where = where :+ condition)\n  \n  def limit(count: Int): QueryBuilder =\n    copy(limit = Some(count))\n  \n  def build: String = {\n    val parts = scala.collection.mutable.ListBuffer[String]()\n    parts += s\"SELECT ${select.mkString(\", \")}\"\n    from.foreach(t => parts += s\"FROM $t\")\n    where.foreach(w => parts += s\"WHERE $w\")\n    limit.foreach(l => parts += s\"LIMIT $l\")\n    parts.mkString(\" \")\n  }\n}\n\n// Usage\nval query = QueryBuilder()\n  .select(\"id\", \"name\")\n  .from(\"users\")\n  .where(\"age > 18\")\n  .limit(10)\n  .build\n// SELECT id, name FROM users WHERE age > 18 LIMIT 10\n\n// Alternative: using companion object with apply\nobject QueryBuilder {\n  def apply(): QueryBuilder = new QueryBuilder()\n  \n  def select(fields: String*): QueryBuilder =\n    QueryBuilder().select(fields: _*)\n}\n\n// Advanced: using implicit classes for DSL\nimplicit class QueryDSL(val self: QueryBuilder) {\n  def and(condition: String): QueryBuilder =\n    self.where(s\"${self.where.last} AND $condition\")\n}\n\nval query = QueryBuilder()\n  .select(\"id\", \"name\")\n  .from(\"users\")\n  .where(\"age > 18\")\n  .and(\"status = 'active'\")\n  .build\n\n// Best practice: sealed case class hierarchy for query types\nsealed trait Query {\n  def build: String\n}\n\ncase class SelectQuery(\n  fields: List[String],\n  table: String,\n  conditions: List[String] = Nil,\n  limitCount: Option[Int] = None\n) extends Query {\n  def build: String = {\n    val parts = scala.collection.mutable.ListBuffer[String]()\n    parts += s\"SELECT ${fields.mkString(\", \")}\"\n    parts += s\"FROM $table\"\n    conditions.foreach(c => parts += s\"WHERE $c\")\n    limitCount.foreach(l => parts += s\"LIMIT $l\")\n    parts.mkString(\" \")\n  }\n}\n\n// Fluent builder for sealed types\nclass QueryBuilder {\n  private var fields: List[String] = Nil\n  private var table: Option[String] = None\n  private var conditions: List[String] = Nil\n  private var limitCount: Option[Int] = None\n  \n  def select(f: String*): this.type = {\n    fields = f.toList\n    this\n  }\n  \n  def from(t: String): this.type = {\n    table = Some(t)\n    this\n  }\n  \n  def where(c: String): this.type = {\n    conditions :+= c\n    this\n  }\n  \n  def limit(n: Int): this.type = {\n    limitCount = Some(n)\n    this\n  }\n  \n  def build: SelectQuery = {\n    SelectQuery(\n      fields = fields,\n      table = table.getOrElse(\"\"),\n      conditions = conditions,\n      limitCount = limitCount\n    )\n  }\n}\n\n// Usage\nval query = new QueryBuilder()\n  .select(\"id\", \"name\")\n  .from(\"users\")\n  .where(\"age > 18\")\n  .limit(10)\n  .build\n```\n\n**Key Points:**\n- Use immutable case classes with `.copy()` for the builder (preferred)\n- Use `this.type` return type for mutable builders to preserve self type\n- Companion objects provide factory methods and default instances\n- For-comprehensions and implicit classes enable DSL-like syntax\n- Sealed trait hierarchies ensure exhaustive pattern matching\n- Builder methods should return `this` or modified copy for chaining\n\n---\n\n## BONUS: COMMON LIBRARIES FOR SCALA 2.13\n\n| Feature | Python | Scala |\n|---------|--------|-------|\n| JSON | `json`, `dataclasses` | `circe`, `play-json` |\n| Testing | `pytest` | `ScalaTest`, `munit` |\n| FP structures | `typing` | `cats`, `scalaz` |\n| Async | `asyncio` | `scala.concurrent.Future`, `cats-effect` |\n| Validation | `pydantic` | `cats.Validated`, `circe` |\n| Immutable data | `dataclasses` | `case class` |\n| Pattern matching | Limited | Native (sealed traits) |\n| Type safety | `mypy` | Scala compiler |\n\n---\n\n## SUMMARY TABLE: Python → Scala 2.13\n\n| Python Concept | Scala Equivalent | Key Difference |\n|---|---|---|\n| `ABC` with generics | `trait` or `abstract class` | Traits are more flexible |\n| `Union[A, B]` | `sealed trait` + case classes | Type-safe with pattern matching |\n| `Optional[T]` | `Option[T]` | Same semantics, better integration |\n| `Protocol` | `trait` | Explicit is better (less structural) |\n| `TypeVar` + bounds | `T <: Bound` | Similar syntax |\n| `@dataclass(frozen=True)` | `case class` | Auto-implements equals, hashCode, toString |\n| `Enum` | `sealed trait` + case objects | More flexible, pattern matching ready |\n| Generators | `LazyList` | Built-in, more functional |\n| Decorators | Function composition | Different paradigm |\n| List/Dict/Set | `List[T]`/`Map[K,V]`/`Set[T]` | Immutable by default |\n| Mutable versions | `mutable.ListBuffer` etc | Explicit opt-in to mutation |\n| List comprehension | `map`/`filter`/`flatMap` or for-comp | Composable, more functional |\n| Builder pattern | case class + `.copy()` | Immutable builders preferred |\n| null handling | `Option`/`Either`/`Try` | No null by default |\n\nThis guide should give you solid Scala 2.13 patterns for all your Python translation batch of tokens for accumulation during processing.\n * Provides state safety with processed flag.\n */\nclass MutableTokenBatch {\n  private var tokens: scala.collection.mutable.ListBuffer[Token] = scala.collection.mutable.ListBuffer.empty\n  private var _processed: Boolean = false\n\n  def add(token: Token): Unit = {\n    if (_processed) {\n      throw new IllegalStateException(\"Batch already processed\")\n    }\n    tokens += token\n  }\n\n  def markProcessed(): Unit = {\n    _processed = true\n  }\n\n  def isProcessed: Boolean = _processed\n\n  def getTokens: List[Token] = */\ntrait TokenContainer[+T] {\n  def getAll: Seq[T]\n\n  def map[U](func: T => String): List[String] =\n    getAll.map(func).toList\n\n  def filter(predicate: T => Boolean): List[T] =\n = _items\n  }\n}\n\n/**\n * Contravariant sink - can accept supertypes.\n * The -T variance allows sink[Supertype] to accept [Subtype].\n */\ntrait TokenSink[-T] {\n  def receive(item: T): Unit\n\n  def drain(): List[T]\n}\n\nobject TokenSink {\n  def apply[T](): TokenSink[T] = new TokenSink[T] {\n    private val received = scala.collection.mutable.ListBuffer.empty[T]\n\n    def receive(item: T): type matching required.\n * T is invariant, allowing both get and set operations.\n */\nclass BivariantHandler[T](initial: T) {\n  private var value: T = initial\n\n  def get: T = value\n\n  def set(newValue: T): Unit = {\n    value = newValue\n  }\n\n  def transform(func: T => T): T = {\n    value = func(value)\n    value\n  }\n}\n\n// ============================================================================\n\n/**\n * Abstract base trait for tokenizers with generic input type.\n * Replaces Python's ABC with @abstractmethod.\n */\ntrait BaseTokenizer[T] {\n  /**\n   * Convert value to token.\n   */\n  def tokenize(value: T): Token\n\n  /**\n   * Lazy tokenization of multiple values using Iterator.\n   */\n  def ============================================================================\n\n/**\n * Tokenizer for string and bytes types.\n *\n * @param encoding Character encoding for bytes\n * @param normalizer Optional function to normalize strings\n */\nclass StringTokenizer(\n  val encoding: String = \"UTF-8\",\n  val normalizer: String => String = identity\n) extends BaseTokenizer[String | Array[Byte]] {\n  def tokenize(value: String | Array[Byte]): Token = {\n    val strValue = value match {\n      case s: String => s\n      case bytes: Array[Byte] => new String(bytes, encoding)\n      case _ => | Double | BigDecimal | BigInt): Token = {\n    val (strValue, originalType) = value match {\n      case i: Int => (i.toString, \"Int\")\n      case l: Long => (l.toString, \"Long\")\n      case d: Double => (f\"$d%.${precision}f\", \"Double\")\n      case bd: BigDecimal => (f\"$bd%.${precision}f\", \"BigDecimal\")\n      case bi: BigInt => (bi.toString, \"BigInt\")\n      case _ => NumericTokenizer =\n    new NumericTokenizer(precision)\n\n  def apply(precision: Int, formatOptions: Map[String, Any]): NumericTokenizer =\n    new NumericTokenizer(precision, def tokenize(value: LocalDateTime | LocalDate): Token = {\n    val formatter = formatStr match {\n      case Some(fmt) => DateTimeFormatter.ofPattern(fmt)\n      case None => value match {\n        case _: LocalDateTime => isoFormat\n        case _: LocalDate => dateFormat\n      }\n    }\n\n    val formatted = value match {\n      case dt: LocalDateTime => dt.format(formatter)\n      case d: pattern matching dispatch\n// ============================================================================\n\n/**\n * Tokenizer that handles multiple types with pattern matching.\n * Replaces Python's @overload methods with Scala's match expressions.\n */\nclass UniversalTokenizer {\n  private val stringTokenizer = StringTokenizer()\n  private val numericTokenizer = NumericTokenizer()\n  private val temporalTokenizer = TemporalTokenizer()\n\n  /**\n   * Dispatch to appropriate tokenizer based on runtime type.\n   * Uses Scala's type-safe pattern matching instead of Python's duck typing.\n   */\n  def tokenize(value: Any): Token = value match {\n    case null => Token(\"NULL\", TokenType.Null)\n\n    case t: Tokenizable => Token(t.toToken, TokenType.Structured)\n\n    case s: String => stringTokenizer.tokenize(s)\n    case b: Array[Byte] => stringTokenizer.tokenize(b)\n\n    case i: Int => numericTokenizer.tokenize(i)\n    case l: Long => numericTokenizer.tokenize(l)\n    case d: Any): Token = value match {\n    case null => Token(\"NULL\", TokenType.Null)\n\n    case t: Tokenizable => Token(t.toToken, TokenType.Structured)\n\n    case s: String => stringTokenizer.tokenize(s)\n    case b: Array[Byte] => stringTokenizer.tokenize(b)\n\n    case i: Int => numericTokenizer.tokenize(i)\n    case l: Long => numericTokenizer.tokenize(l)\n    case d: Double => numericTokenizer.tokenize(d)\n    case bd: BigDecimal => numericTokenizer.tokenize(bd)\n    case bi: BigInt => numericTokenizer.tokenize(bi)\n\n    case dt: LocalDateTime => temporalTokenizer.tokenize(dt)\n    case d: LocalDate => temporalTokenizer.tokenize(d)\n\n    // Fallback: convert to string\n    case other => Token(other.toString, TokenType.String, Map(\"fallback\" -> true))\n  }\n\n  /**\n   * Tokenize multiple values of mixed types.\n   */\n  def tokenizeMixed(values: Iterable[Any]): List[Token] =\n    values.map(tokenize).toList\n}\n\nobject with complex nested generics\n// ============================================================================\n\n/**\n * Registry with complex nested generic types.\n * Manages containers and handlers for type-generic token processing.\n */\nclass TokenRegistry[T] {\n  private val registry: scala.collection.mutable.Map[String, TokenContainer[T]] =\n    scala.collection.mutable.Map.empty\n  private val handlers: scala.collection.mutable.ListBuffer[T => Option[Token]] =\n    scala.collection.mutable.ListBuffer.empty\n\n  def register(key: String, container: TokenContainer[T]): Unit = {\n    registry(key) = container\n  }\n\n  def addHandler(handler: T => Option[Token]): Unit = {\n    handlers += handler\n  }\n\n  /**\n   * Process all items in a container through all handlers.\n   * Some(container) =>\n        val results = scala.collection.mutable.ListBuffer.empty[Option[Token]]\n\n        for (item <- container.getAll) {\n          var handled = false\n          for (handler <- handlers if !handled) {\n            handler(item) match {\n              case Some(token) =>\n                results += Some(token)\n                handled = true\n              case None => ()\n            }\n          }\n          if (!handled) {\n            results += None\n          }\n        }\n        results.toList\n    }\n  }\n\n  def getRegistry: Map[String, TokenContainer[T]] = registry.toMap\n}\n\n// ============================================================================\n// Functor and Monad for functional token processing\n// */\ntrait TokenFunctor[T] {\n  def value: T\n\n  def map[U](func: T => U): TokenFunctor[U] =\n    TokenFunctorImpl(func(value))\n\n  def flatMap[U](func: T => TokenFunctor[U]): TokenFunctor[U] =\n    func(value)\n\n  def getOrElse(default: T): T =\n    if (value == null) default else value\n}\n\ncase class TokenFunctorImpl[T](value: T) extends TokenFunctor[T]\n\nobject TokenFunctor {\n  def ============================================================================\n\n/**\n * Tokenizer for JSON structures with recursive types.\n * Uses Scala's for-comprehension and pattern matching for JSON traversal.\n */\nclass JsonTokenizer(val pretty: Boolean = false) {\n\n  private def jsonToString(value: JsonValue): String = {\n    // Simple JSON serialization\n    value match {\n      case null => \"null\"\n      case b: Boolean => b.toString\n      case s: String => s\"\"\"\"$s\"\"\"\"\n      case i: Int => i.toString\n      case l: Long => l.toString\n      case d: Double => d.toString\n      case bd: BigDecimal => bd.toString\n      case bi: BigInt => bi.toString\n      case seq: Seq[_] =>\n        val items = seq.map(v => jsonToString(v.asInstanceOf[JsonValue]))\n        if (pretty) \"[\\n  \" + items.mkString(\",\\n  \") + \"\\n]\"\n        else \"[\" + items.mkString(\", \") + \"]\"\n      case map: Map[_, _] =>\n        val items = map.asInstanceOf[Map[String, JsonValue]].map { case (k, v) =>\n          s\"\"\"\"$k\": ${jsonToString(v)}\"\"\"\n        }\n        if (pretty) \"{\\n  \" + items.mkString(\",\\n  \") + \"\\n}\"\n        else \"{\" + TokenType.Structured, Map(\"json\" -> true))\n  }\n\n  /**\n   * Extract and tokenize a value at a JSON path.\n   * Path format: \"field.nested.0.array_item\"\n   */\n  def tokenizePath(value: JsonValue, path: String): Option[Token] = {\n    val parts = path.split(\"\\\\.\")\n\n    def traverse(current: JsonValue, parts: List[String]): Option[JsonValue] = {\n      parts match {\n        case Nil => Some(current)\n        case head :: tail =>\n          current match {\n            case map: Map[String @unchecked, JsonValue @unchecked] =>\n              map.get(head).flatMap(v => traverse(v, tail))\n            case seq: Seq[JsonValue @unchecked] if head.forall(_.isDigit) =>\n              val idx = head.toInt\n              if (idx >= 0 && idx < seq.length)\n handlers if !handled) {\n            handler(item) match {\n              case Some(token) =>\n                results += Some(token)\n                handled = true\n              case None => ()\n ============================================================================\n// JSON Tokenization with recursive types\n// ============================================================================\n\n/**\n * Tokenizer for JSON structures with recursive types.\n * Uses Scala's for-comprehension and pattern matching for JSON traversal.\n */\nclass JsonTokenizer(val pretty: Boolean = false) {\n\n  private def jsonToString(value: Any): String = {\n    // Simple JSON serialization\n    value match {\n      case null => \"null\"\n      case b: Boolean => b.toString\n      case s: String => s\"\"\"\"$s\"\"\"\"\n      case i: Int => i.toString\n      case l: Long => l.toString\n      case d: Double => d.toString\n      case bd: BigDecimal => bd.toString\n      case bi: BigInt => bi.toString\n      case seq: Seq[_] =>\n        val items = seq.map(v => jsonToString(v))\n        if (pretty) \"[\\n  \" + items.mkString(\",\\n  \") + \"\\n]\"\n        else \"[\" + items.mkString(\", \") + \"]\"\n      case map: Map[_, _] =>\n        val items = map.map { case (k, v) =>\n          s\"\"\"\"$k\": ${jsonToString(v)}\"\"\"\n        }\n        if (pretty) \"{\\n  \" + items.mkString(\",\\n  \") + \"\\n}\"\n        else \"{\" + items.mkString(\", \") + \"}\"\n      case _ => value.toString\n    }\n  }\n\n  def tokenize(value: Any): Token = {\n    val jsonStr = jsonToString(value)\n    Token(jsonStr, TokenType.Structured, Map(\"json\" -> true))\n  }\n\n  /**\n   * Extract and tokenize a value at a JSON path.\n   * Path format: \"field.nested.0.array_item\"\n   */\n  def tokenizePath(value: Any, path: String): Option[Token] = {\n    val parts = path.split(\"\\\\.\")\n\n    def traverse(current: Any, parts: List[String]): Option[Any] = {\n      parts match {\n        case Nil => Some(current)\n        case head :: tail =>\n          current match {\n            case map: Map[_, _] =>\n              map.asInstanceOf[Map[String, Any]].get(head).flatMap(v => traverse(v, tail))\n            case seq: Seq[_] if head.forall(_.isDigit) =>\n              val idx = head.toInt\n              if (idx >= 0 && idx < seq.length)\n                traverse(seq(idx), tail)\n              else None\n            case _ => None\n          }\n      }\n    }\n\n    traverse(value, parts.toList).map(tokenize)\n  }\n}\n\nobject JsonTokenizer {\n  def apply(pretty: Boolean = false): JsonTokenizer = new JsonTokenizer(pretty)\n}\n\n// pattern matching, exhaustiveness checking by compiler\n\n### 2. **Immutable Data Classes**\n- **Python:** `@dataclass(frozen=True) class Token`\n- **Scala:** `case class Token(...)`\n- **Benefit:** Auto-generated equals, hashCode, toString, copy method; immutability by default\n\n### 3. **Abstract Base Classes with Generics**\n- **Python:** `class BaseTokenizer(ABC, Generic[T])` with `@abstractmethod`\n- **Scala:** `trait BaseTokenizer[T]` with abstract methods\n- **Benefit:** Traits provide more flexibility than abstract classes; multiple inheritance support\n\n### 4. **Variance Annotations**\n- **Python:** `TypeVar(\"T_co\", covariant=True)`, `TypeVar(\"T_contra\", contravariant=True)`\n- **Scala:** `trait TokenContainer[+T]` (covariant), `trait TokenSink[-T]` (contravariant)\n- **Benefit:** Compiler-enforced type safety; covariance/contravariance at definition site\n\n### 5. **Union Types & Option Handling**\n- **Python:** `Union[X, Y]`, `Optional[T]`\n- **Scala:** `sealed trait` hierarchies, `Option[T]`, `Either[Error, T]`\n- **Benefit:** Type-safe pattern matching replaces runtime isinstance checks\n\n### 6. **Builder Pattern**\n- **Python:** Mutable builder with method chaining\n- **Scala:** Immutable `case class TokenizerBuilder[T]` with `.copy()` semantics\n- **Benefit:** Thread-safe by default; functional composition style\n\n### 7. **Higher-Kinded Types (Functors/Monads)**\n- **Python:** Simulated with `Generic[T]` and method overloading\n- **Scala:** Proper `trait TokenFunctor[T]` with `map` and `flatMap` implementations\n- **Benefit:** Compatible with functional libraries like Cats; supports monadic operations\n\n### 8. **Protocols & Structural Typing**\n- **Python:** `@runtime_checkable class Tokenizable(Protocol)`\n- **Scala:** `trait Tokenizable` with explicit implementation\n- **Benefit:** Explicit contracts preferred over structural typing; better IDE support\n\n## All Translated Components\n\n### Core Types\n- `TokenType` (sealed trait with case objects: String, Numeric, Temporal, Structured, Binary, Null)\n- `Token` (immutable case class with metadata)\n- `Tokenizable` (trait for type conversion)\n\n### Mutable Collections\n- `MutableTokenBatch` (for accumulating tokens with state safety)\n\n### Generic Containers\n- `TokenContainer[+T]` (covariant, producer-oriented)\n- `TokenSink[-T]` (contravariant, consumer-oriented)\n- `BivariantHandler[T]` (invariant, bidirectional)\n\n### Base Tokenizers (trait hierarchy)\n- `BaseTokenizer[T]` (abstract base with generic type)\n- `StringTokenizer` (handles String and Array[Byte])\n- `NumericTokenizer` (Int, Long, Double, BigDecimal, BigInt with precision)\n- `TemporalTokenizer` (LocalDateTime, LocalDate with custom format support)\n\n### Universal Tokenizer\n- `UniversalTokenizer` (pattern matching dispatch for multiple types)\n- Uses sealed trait pattern matching instead of Python's isinstance checks\n\n### Advanced
